========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.

I’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.

Usually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.

After one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.

And then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.

Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.

For the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.

Some weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.

It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these nights that feel really out of control with food. It usually starts kind of normally, like I’ll get home from school and I’m grading papers or planning lessons, and I’ll have leftovers or something. But then it just keeps going.\n\nLike, I’ll finish that and then suddenly I’m eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter straight from the jar, and then I’ll remember I have snacks I packed for my classroom and I’ll go through those too. It’s just… the amount scares me afterward, and while it’s happening I feel like I can’t really stop it, even though part of me is watching it happen and thinking, “What are you doing?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely don’t feel good, but “guilty” is maybe… I’m not sure if that’s the right word or if I’m just embarrassed and upset with myself. It’s kind of all tangled together.\n\nUsually once it’s over, I sort of come back to myself and I’m looking at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever, and I just feel this heavy feeling, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if that counts as guilt in the way you mean it. It’s not like I’ve sat there and labeled it that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.

For the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.

And if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.

It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.

There have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.

Part of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.

I’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.

Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up on purpose after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.\n\nThere have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these nights that feel really out of control with food. It usually starts kind of normally, like I’ll get home from school and I’m grading papers or planning lessons, and I’ll have leftovers or something. But then it just keeps going.\n\nLike, I’ll finish that and then suddenly I’m eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter straight from the jar, and then I’ll remember I have snacks I packed for my classroom and I’ll go through those too. It’s just… the amount scares me afterward, and while it’s happening I feel like I can’t really stop it, even though part of me is watching it happen and thinking, “What are you doing?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely don’t feel good, but “guilty” is maybe… I’m not sure if that’s the right word or if I’m just embarrassed and upset with myself. It’s kind of all tangled together.\n\nUsually once it’s over, I sort of come back to myself and I’m looking at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever, and I just feel this heavy feeling, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if that counts as guilt in the way you mean it. It’s not like I’ve sat there and labeled it that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up on purpose after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.\n\nThere have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating more than once?\n\nPatient: I… I honestly don’t know how to answer that in a way that’s both honest and not… I don’t know, not making it sound more definite than I can handle saying.\n\nWhen you say “more than once,” my brain kind of freezes, because then it feels like I have to count something or admit there’s a pattern, and I’m not sure I’m ready to do that out loud. I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating more than once?\n\nPatient: I… I honestly don’t know how to answer that in a way that’s both honest and not… I don’t know, not making it sound more definite than I can handle saying.\n\nWhen you say “more than once,” my brain kind of freezes, because then it feels like I have to count something or admit there’s a pattern, and I’m not sure I’m ready to do that out loud. I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these nights that feel really out of control with food. It usually starts kind of normally, like I’ll get home from school and I’m grading papers or planning lessons, and I’ll have leftovers or something. But then it just keeps going.\n\nLike, I’ll finish that and then suddenly I’m eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter straight from the jar, and then I’ll remember I have snacks I packed for my classroom and I’ll go through those too. It’s just… the amount scares me afterward, and while it’s happening I feel like I can’t really stop it, even though part of me is watching it happen and thinking, “What are you doing?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely don’t feel good, but “guilty” is maybe… I’m not sure if that’s the right word or if I’m just embarrassed and upset with myself. It’s kind of all tangled together.\n\nUsually once it’s over, I sort of come back to myself and I’m looking at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever, and I just feel this heavy feeling, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if that counts as guilt in the way you mean it. It’s not like I’ve sat there and labeled it that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up on purpose after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.\n\nThere have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating more than once?\n\nPatient: I… I honestly don’t know how to answer that in a way that’s both honest and not… I don’t know, not making it sound more definite than I can handle saying.\n\nWhen you say “more than once,” my brain kind of freezes, because then it feels like I have to count something or admit there’s a pattern, and I’m not sure I’m ready to do that out loud. I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. I can tell you that it’s happened around those episodes that I’ve had that feeling and that bathroom moment more than once. But I don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating more than once in the same week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. Like, the “same week” part makes me feel like I have to go back and sort through days and count, and honestly when those episodes happen, everything feels kind of blurry afterward. I’m usually not calmly keeping track of what happened when.\n\nI know there have been weeks where things felt really bad, where I was scared of eating and then would end up feeling out of control and ashamed, and I know I’ve had more than one of those panicky bathroom moments around that. But saying for sure, “yes, more than once in the same week,” I don’t know. I don’t want to guess and then feel like I’m exaggerating, but I also don’t want to pretend it’s never gotten that bad.\n\nI’m sorry. I can tell you it has felt like a repeated thing at times, not just one isolated moment, but I don’t have a clear count or a clear week in my head right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked me a few times, and I’m sorry that I keep circling around it instead of just answering the way you need me to.\n\nI think… I’m still not sure how to say it plainly. There have been times after eating where I felt completely panicked and ashamed and went to the bathroom with that feeling of needing to undo what just happened. That part I can say. But when you ask, “Have you made yourself throw up,” I just kind of lock up, because saying yes feels really hard and saying no doesn’t feel fully honest either.\n\nI’m not trying to be evasive. I think I’m just embarrassed and scared of what it means if I admit it clearly. I can tell you that this has been part of what happens around eating sometimes, that feeling of trying to get rid of it afterward, but I don’t feel able to give you a clean answer right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. I can tell you that it’s happened around those episodes that I’ve had that feeling and that bathroom moment more than once. But I don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating more than once in the same week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. Like, the “same week” part makes me feel like I have to go back and sort through days and count, and honestly when those episodes happen, everything feels kind of blurry afterward. I’m usually not calmly keeping track of what happened when.\n\nI know there have been weeks where things felt really bad, where I was scared of eating and then would end up feeling out of control and ashamed, and I know I’ve had more than one of those panicky bathroom moments around that. But saying for sure, “yes, more than once in the same week,” I don’t know. I don’t want to guess and then feel like I’m exaggerating, but I also don’t want to pretend it’s never gotten that bad.\n\nI’m sorry. I can tell you it has felt like a repeated thing at times, not just one isolated moment, but I don’t have a clear count or a clear week in my head right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked me a few times, and I’m sorry that I keep circling around it instead of just answering the way you need me to.\n\nI think… I’m still not sure how to say it plainly. There have been times after eating where I felt completely panicked and ashamed and went to the bathroom with that feeling of needing to undo what just happened. That part I can say.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know if I can put it into “more often than once a month” in a reliable way. I know that probably sounds frustrating, because you’re trying to understand the frequency, and I keep giving you these vague answers.

It’s just that I don’t really track it like that. Or maybe I avoid tracking it, honestly. When something happens after eating, it’s not like I’m marking it on a calendar. I’m usually trying not to think about it afterward. So when you ask if it’s more than once a month, part of me thinks it might be, at least during periods when things are worse, but I don’t feel confident saying that as a clear fact.

I can say it hasn’t felt like just one random incident that never came back. It feels like something that has repeated enough that I’m scared of the pattern. But if you need me to say, “yes, definitely more than once a month,” I’m not sure I can say that without feeling like I’m guessing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these nights that feel really out of control with food. It usually starts kind of normally, like I’ll get home from school and I’m grading papers or planning lessons, and I’ll have leftovers or something. But then it just keeps going.\n\nLike, I’ll finish that and then suddenly I’m eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter straight from the jar, and then I’ll remember I have snacks I packed for my classroom and I’ll go through those too. It’s just… the amount scares me afterward, and while it’s happening I feel like I can’t really stop it, even though part of me is watching it happen and thinking, “What are you doing?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely don’t feel good, but “guilty” is maybe… I’m not sure if that’s the right word or if I’m just embarrassed and upset with myself. It’s kind of all tangled together.\n\nUsually once it’s over, I sort of come back to myself and I’m looking at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever, and I just feel this heavy feeling, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if that counts as guilt in the way you mean it. It’s not like I’ve sat there and labeled it that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up on purpose after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.\n\nThere have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating more than once?\n\nPatient: I… I honestly don’t know how to answer that in a way that’s both honest and not… I don’t know, not making it sound more definite than I can handle saying.\n\nWhen you say “more than once,” my brain kind of freezes, because then it feels like I have to count something or admit there’s a pattern, and I’m not sure I’m ready to do that out loud. I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. I can tell you that it’s happened around those episodes that I’ve had that feeling and that bathroom moment more than once. I don’t know that I can honestly say “at least once a week” as a definite thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s the exact kind of answer you’re trying to get, and I keep not giving you a clean number.

It’s more that there are stretches where it feels like it’s happening too much, and then other times where I try very hard not to think about it or I sort of lose track. The weeks blur together, especially because afterward I don’t sit there and record it or anything—I mostly feel embarrassed and want to act like it didn’t happen. So when you ask “once a week,” my brain immediately starts trying to count and then I get stuck.

I can say it’s not something I feel comfortable dismissing as rare or meaningless. It feels like enough of a pattern that I’m scared of it. But I don’t feel able to confidently say, yes, every week, or at least once a week, because I’m not sure I actually know that clearly.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these nights that feel really out of control with food. It usually starts kind of normally, like I’ll get home from school and I’m grading papers or planning lessons, and I’ll have leftovers or something. But then it just keeps going.\n\nLike, I’ll finish that and then suddenly I’m eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter straight from the jar, and then I’ll remember I have snacks I packed for my classroom and I’ll go through those too. It’s just… the amount scares me afterward, and while it’s happening I feel like I can’t really stop it, even though part of me is watching it happen and thinking, “What are you doing?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely don’t feel good, but “guilty” is maybe… I’m not sure if that’s the right word or if I’m just embarrassed and upset with myself. It’s kind of all tangled together.\n\nUsually once it’s over, I sort of come back to myself and I’m looking at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever, and I just feel this heavy feeling, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t know if that counts as guilt in the way you mean it. It’s not like I’ve sat there and labeled it that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the scariest part, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, like having an extra snack or whatever. It feels like once I start, something kind of switches over, and the reasonable part of me—the part that can manage a classroom full of kids and keep everyone on schedule—is just watching from the outside.\n\nI’ll literally think, Joan, stop. Put it away. You don’t even want this anymore. And at the same time my hands are still opening another package or getting a spoon or going back to the cabinet. It feels almost ridiculous to say, because I know I’m the one doing it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I can just decide to stop and then stop.\n\nUsually I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, like physically painful, and then it’s like I finally sort of snap out of it. But by then it’s already happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not usually by, um, making myself throw up or anything like that. But I definitely try to “fix” it the next day, I guess.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up feeling panicky and kind of disgusted with myself, and I’ll make these rules in my head, like, Okay, you don’t get breakfast today. And then I’ll pack my lunch for school but tell myself I’m not allowed to eat it, or I’ll just bring coffee and pretend I’m too busy to stop. Which is easy to hide because teaching is so nonstop anyway—there’s always a meeting or a kid who needs something or copies to make—so I can tell myself I’m being productive, but really I’m punishing myself.\n\nAnd then after school I’ll go out on the trail and stay there for hours, way longer than I planned, or I’ll do extra yoga at home even if I’m shaky and exhausted. Sometimes I’m not even enjoying it, I’m just thinking I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… often. More often than I really want to admit out loud.\n\nFor the past few months, I don’t think there’s been many weeks where it hasn’t happened at least once. Usually there’s at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of go into damage-control mode. Like I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school, or I’ll just have coffee and keep moving so I don’t have to think about it. Or I’ll make myself do a really long walk after work, or extra yoga, even if I’m tired.\n\nSome weeks it’s more than once, but I’m a little afraid to count it too carefully, if that makes sense. It’s become enough of a pattern that I’m kind of always aware of it in the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I notice my body all the time—probably too much—but I haven’t been keeping track in a clear way, like writing down numbers or anything.\n\nSome days my clothes feel different, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight changing or just me feeling bloated or uncomfortable or being hyper-aware of everything. I’ve avoided weighing myself sometimes because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I’ll want to check but then I get in my head about it.\n\nSo… significant changes? I don’t know. I can’t say for sure. Nothing where someone else has commented or anything like that, at least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: How often do you try to compensate for these episodes of eating by restricting food or exercising?\n\nPatient: Pretty much whenever it happens, honestly. Like… if I have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, the next day I almost automatically go into this “make up for it” mindset.\n\nFor the past few months, there’s rarely been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Usually there’s at least one night, sometimes more, where I feel like I’ve gone too far, and then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not going to eat at school. I’ll bring something and not touch it, or I’ll just have coffee and keep saying I’m too busy, even though part of me knows I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd if I don’t restrict like that, or sometimes even if I do, I’ll push myself to go on a really long walk after work, or do extra yoga at home. It can feel less like exercise and more like… I have to do it because of what I ate. Like I’m trying to balance the scales or erase it somehow. So I guess the short answer is at least weekly, and usually tied directly to those nights.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Do you mean like, deliberately trying to make it happen, not just feeling sick or panicky afterward?\n\nI know after those episodes I get really overwhelmed and ashamed, and I start thinking about how to undo it somehow. But with throwing up specifically, I feel kind of stuck even talking about it. It’s embarrassing, and I’m not sure I can give you a clear answer right this second.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up on purpose after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking. I’m just… I’m having a hard time saying yes or no in a straightforward way.\n\nThere have definitely been times after eating like that where I felt so uncomfortable and ashamed that I wanted my body to just get rid of it somehow. Like I’d be in the bathroom thinking about it, panicking, feeling gross, feeling like I’d ruined everything. But whether I’ve actually made myself throw up on purpose… I don’t know how to talk about that clearly right now.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “No, not really,” because I don’t want that to be true, and because it feels really embarrassing to even imagine saying it out loud. But part of me also knows that I get into these moments where I’m not thinking normally and I’m just desperate to undo what happened.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a clean answer. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just feel really exposed answering that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating more than once?\n\nPatient: I… I honestly don’t know how to answer that in a way that’s both honest and not… I don’t know, not making it sound more definite than I can handle saying.\n\nWhen you say “more than once,” my brain kind of freezes, because then it feels like I have to count something or admit there’s a pattern, and I’m not sure I’m ready to do that out loud. I know there have been more than one times where I’ve ended up in the bathroom after eating too much and felt panicked and disgusted with myself, and like I needed to undo it somehow. But I’m still having a really hard time saying plainly, “yes, I made myself throw up,” even if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to hide things from you. It’s more like… the second I try to put exact words to it, I feel ashamed and kind of trapped. I can tell you that it’s happened around those episodes that I’ve had that feeling and that bathroom moment more than once. But I don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating more than once in the same week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. Like, the “same week” part makes me feel like I have to go back and sort through days and count, and honestly when those episodes happen, everything feels kind of blurry afterward. I’m usually not calmly keeping track of what happened when.\n\nI know there have been weeks where things felt really bad, where I was scared of eating and then would end up feeling out of control and ashamed, and I know I’ve had more than one of those panicky bathroom moments around that. But saying for sure, “yes, more than once in the same week,” I don’t know. I don’t want to guess and then feel like I’m exaggerating, but I also don’t want to pretend it’s never gotten that bad.\n\nI’m sorry. I can tell you it has felt like a repeated thing at times, not just one isolated moment, but I don’t have a clear count or a clear week in my head right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked me a few times, and I’m sorry that I keep circling around it instead of just answering the way you need me to.\n\nI think… I’m still not sure how to say it plainly. There have been times after eating where I felt completely panicked and ashamed and went to the bathroom with that feeling of needing to undo what just happened. That part I can say. But when you ask, “Have you made yourself throw up,” I just kind of lock up, because saying yes feels really hard and saying no doesn’t feel fully honest either.\n\nI’m not trying to be evasive. I think I’m just embarrassed and scared of what it means if I admit it clearly. I can tell you that this has been part of what happens around eating sometimes, that feeling of trying to get rid of it afterward, but I don’t feel able to give you a clean answer right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating more often than once a month?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I can put it into “more often than once a month” in a reliable way. I know that probably sounds frustrating, because you’re trying to understand the frequency, and I keep giving you these vague answers.\n\nIt’s just that I don’t really track it like that. Or maybe I avoid tracking it, honestly. When something happens after eating, it’s not like I’m marking it on a calendar. I’m usually trying not to think about it afterward. So when you ask if it’s more than once a month, part of me thinks it might be, at least during periods when things are worse, but I don’t feel confident saying that as a clear fact.\n\nI can say it hasn’t felt like just one random incident that never came back. It feels like something that has repeated enough that I’m scared of the pattern. But if you need me to say, “yes, definitely more than once a month,” I’m not sure I can say that without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating at least once a week?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say “at least once a week” as a definite thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s the exact kind of answer you’re trying to get, and I keep not giving you a clean number.\n\nIt’s more that there are stretches where it feels like it’s happening too much, and then other times where I try very hard not to think about it or I sort of lose track. The weeks blur together, especially because afterward I don’t sit there and record it or anything—I mostly feel embarrassed and want to act like it didn’t happen. So when you ask “once a week,” my brain immediately starts trying to count and then I get stuck.\n\nI can say it’s not something I feel comfortable dismissing as rare or meaningless. It feels like enough of a pattern that I’m scared of it. But I don’t feel able to confidently say, yes, every week, or at least once a week, because I’m not sure I actually know that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating at all?\n\nPatient: I’m still struggling to answer that directly, even though I know you’re asking it in the most basic way now.\n\nI think the most honest thing I can say is that after eating, there have been times where I’ve felt like I needed to get rid of it, or undo it somehow, and I’ve ended up in the bathroom feeling really upset and ashamed. But when you ask me to say clearly, “Do you make yourself throw up at all,” I feel like my whole body just tightens up. It’s like if I say it out loud, then it becomes this real thing I can’t take back.\n\nI’m not trying to play word games with you. I know it probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, and maybe I am, but not because I don’t want to cooperate. I’m just… embarrassed. And scared. I can tell you that this question feels very close to something I don’t feel ready to put into a simple yes-or-no answer yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
